<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We construct two distinct uniformly positive bounded measurable scalar conductivities on a ball in ℝ<sup>3</sup> with the same full Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator. Both conductivities equal one near the boundary. This gives nonuniqueness in the scalar Calderón problem at bounded measurable regularity.

- **claim-main-nonuniqueness** (counterexample, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): There exist real scalar functions $\gamma_0,\gamma_1\in L^\infty(B(0,3);\mathbb R)$ and constants $0<c<C<\infty$ such that* 1. *$c\leq\gamma_j\leq C$ almost everywhere, for $j=0,1$;* 2. *both conductivities equal $1$ in a neighborhood of the boundary;* 3. *$\left\lvert \{x:\gamma_0(x)\ne\gamma_1(x)\}\right\rvert>0$;* 4. *$\Lambda_{\gamma_0}=\Lambda_{\gamma_1}$ as bounded operators from $H^{1/2}(\partial B(0,3))$ to $H^{-1/2}(\partial B(0,3))$.
  - Explanation: Two bounded scalar conductivities on a three-dimensional ball are bounded between positive constants, equal one near the boundary and distinct on a set of positive measure. They nevertheless induce the same full weak Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator, so uniqueness fails at bounded measurable regularity.
- **claim-localized-nonuniqueness** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-localized)): Let $c,C$ be the constants in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. For every bounded connected Lipschitz domain $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^3$ and every finite family of pairwise disjoint balls $B_1,\ldots,B_m\Subset\Omega$, $m\geq1$, there are $2^m$ pairwise distinct real scalar conductivities $\gamma_\varepsilon\in L^\infty(\Omega)$, indexed by $\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}^m$, such that $$c\leq\gamma_\varepsilon\leq C\quad\text{almost everywhere}, \qquad \gamma_\varepsilon=1\quad\text{on } \Omega\setminus\bigcup_{i=1}^m B_i,$$ and all their full weak Dirichlet-to-Neumann operators from $H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)$ to $H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega)$ agree. Distinct conductivities differ on a set of positive measure.
  - Explanation: The ball counterexample is copied into separated small balls inside any bounded connected Lipschitz domain. Choosing the original or transformed pair independently in each ball gives many pairwise distinct conductivities that share one boundary operator.
- **claim-exact-scalarization** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-scalarization)): Let $U\subset\mathbb R^3$ be a bounded Lipschitz domain. Let $A$ be a measurable symmetric matrix field satisfying $$c_0I\leq A\leq C_0I\qquad\text{almost everywhere in }U, \qquad 0<c_0\leq C_0<\infty.$$ Suppose the real-valued potentials $u_1,u_2\in H^1(U)$ satisfy $\mathop{\mathrm{div}}(A\nabla u_j)=0$. Assume that there is an open cover of a full-measure subset of $U$ on whose members $A,u_1,u_2$ are smooth and have the following local rank property. On each member, a fixed subset of the potentials has everywhere independent gradients, and every remaining potential is a constant affine combination of that subset. The subset may have zero, one, or two elements; a zero-element subset means that both potentials are constant there.* *There exist constants $0<a<b<\infty$, a measurable scalar $a\leq\gamma\leq b$, and $v_1,v_2\in H^1(U)$ such that $$\begin{aligned} \mathop{\mathrm{div}}(\gamma\nabla v_j)&=0, &v_j-u_j&\in H^1_0(U), \label{sc:conclusion-equations}\\ \int_U\gamma\nabla v_j\cdot\nabla\psi\,\,\mathrm dx &=\int_U A\nabla u_j\cdot\nabla\psi\,\,\mathrm dx &&\text{for every }\psi\in H^1(U). \label{sc:conclusion-flux} \end{aligned}$$ The bounds $a,b$ may be chosen using only $c_0,C_0$.
  - Explanation: A uniformly elliptic symmetric matrix conductivity satisfying a local rank condition can be replaced by one bounded measurable scalar conductivity. The replacement keeps the boundary trace and the entire normal-flux functional of each of the two designated solutions. The ellipticity bounds depend only on the original bounds.
- **claim-laminate-class-open** (theorem, [source](#stmt-sc-open-class)): The set $\mathcal G^{\mathrm{diag}}$ is open in $\mathbb R^3$, and $\mathcal G$ is open in $\mathrm{Sym}_3$. Every joining path in a representing tree lies in $\mathcal G$. Given $c_0,C_0$, the numbers $a,b$ can be chosen so that every symmetric matrix with spectrum in $[c_0,C_0]$ belongs to $\mathcal G$.
  - Explanation: The class of finite coordinate laminates built from scalar leaves is open, joining paths stay inside it, and with suitable bounds it contains every symmetric matrix whose spectrum lies in a prescribed interval.
- **claim-polynomial-potential** (theorem, [source](#stmt-sc-polynomial-potential)): For every $\xi\ne0$, $D(\xi)$ is onto. There is a homogeneous polynomial map $P(\xi):V_m\to V_m$ of degree $2m$ such that $$D(\xi)P(\xi)=0,\qquad \operatorname{im}P(\xi)=\ker D(\xi)\quad(\xi\ne0).$$ Consequently every mean-zero smooth periodic plane wave with polarization in $\ker D(\xi)$ admits a compactly supported localization satisfying both constraints exactly, with arbitrarily small absolute uniform norm of the difference from its cut-off leading wave.
  - Explanation: The constraint symbol is onto at every nonzero frequency, and an explicit homogeneous polynomial map parametrizes its kernel. Hence periodic plane waves in the kernel can be localized with compact support while keeping the differential constraints exact.
- **claim-symmetric-correction** (theorem, [source](#stmt-sc-symmetric-correction)): Let $E$ have independent columns and let $R$ satisfy that $E^tR$ is symmetric. With $E^\dagger=(E^tE)^{-1}E^t$, the matrix $$\label{sc:correction-formula} H=R E^\dagger+(E^\dagger)^tR^t -(E^\dagger)^t(E^tR)E^\dagger$$ is symmetric and satisfies $HE=R$. Its norm is bounded by a locally uniform constant times $\left\lVert R\right\rVert$ when $E$ ranges over a bounded set with its smallest singular value bounded below.
  - Explanation: When the pairing of the gradient matrix with a residual is symmetric, an explicit symmetric matrix maps the gradients to that residual, with norm controlled by the residual on bounded sets with a lower singular-value bound.
- **claim-local-join** (theorem, [source](#stmt-sc-local-join)): Let $(A,E,F)$, with potentials $u_1,u_2$, be a subsolution. Let $y_0$ lie in one of its smooth patches from Definition <a href="#sc:subsolution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:subsolution">5</a>, on which one fixed set of $m\in\{1,2\}$ potentials has independent gradients. Suppose $A_0=A(y_0)$ is a coordinate join of two children $A^+,A^-$ in one orthonormal frame, with positive weights $\theta_+,\theta_-$ summing to one and with its joining path contained in $\mathcal G$. Given open neighborhoods $\mathcal O_+,\mathcal O_-$ of the children in $\mathrm{Sym}_3$ and $\delta>0$, there is an open neighborhood $V$ of $y_0$, compactly contained in the patch, with the following property.* *For every open rectangular box $Q$ with $\overline Q\subset V$, one can choose a cutoff and periodic waveform, then construct subsolutions $(A_k',E_k',F_k')$, with potentials $u'_{1,k},u'_{2,k}$, for all sufficiently large integers $k$ such that $A_k'=A$ outside $Q$. The differences $u'_{j,k}-u_j$ and $F'_{j,k}-F_j$ vanish outside compact subsets of $Q$, and $$\int_U(F'_{j,k}-F_j)\cdot\nabla\psi\,\,\mathrm dx=0 \qquad\bigl(\psi\in H^1(U),\ j=1,2\bigr).$$ There are disjoint open subsets $Q_{k,+},Q_{k,-}\subset Q$ such that $$A_k'\in\mathcal O_s\ \hbox{on }Q_{k,s}\quad(s\in\{+,-\}),\qquad \left\lvert Q\setminus(Q_{k,+}\cup Q_{k,-})\right\rvert<\delta\left\lvert Q\right\rvert.$$ On the same fixed box, with the cutoff and waveform fixed, $$(E_k',F_k')\rightharpoonup(E,F) \quad\text{in }L^2(U)\times L^2(U)\quad\text{as }k\to\infty.$$ The neighborhood and frequency threshold may depend on all preceding local data, including $\mathcal O_+,\mathcal O_-$ and $\delta$; the threshold may also depend on the chosen box, cutoff, and waveform.
  - Explanation: On small boxes a coordinate join of two child matrices is realized by high-frequency localized perturbations of a subsolution. The matrix lies near the children except on a set of small volume, boundary data are untouched, and the fields converge weakly to the old pair as the frequency grows.
- **claim-weak-approximation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-sc-weak-approximation)): Given a subsolution, a weak $L^2$ neighborhood of its pair $(E,F)$, and $\varepsilon>0$, there is a subsolution in that neighborhood whose matrix has distance less than $\varepsilon$ from the scalar matrices outside a set of volume less than $\varepsilon$.
  - Explanation: Iterating the local join over finitely many depths gives a subsolution inside any prescribed weak neighborhood whose matrix is close to scalar outside a set of small volume.
- **claim-baire-continuity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-sc-baire)): The identity from $Z$ with its weak topology to $\mathcal H$ with its norm topology has a dense set of continuity points.
  - Explanation: By a Baire category argument on the weakly compact metrizable closure of subsolution pairs, the identity into the norm topology has a dense set of continuity points. Such points yield strong limits.
- **claim-branching-block** (construction, [source](#stmt-prop-branching-block)): Let $B\subset\mathbb R^3$ be a bounded connected Lipschitz domain whose boundary has three components $\Gamma_0,\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2$. Suppose these components have disjoint product collars parametrized by $\mathbb T^2\times[0,\eta_i]$, where $\mathbb T^2=(\mathbb R/2\pi\mathbb Z)^2$. Assume the parametrizations are bi-Lipschitz, smooth with nonsingular derivative on open pieces covering up to a null set, and that removing sufficiently thin collars leaves a connected Lipschitz central region. Let $\mu_i$ be the probability measure obtained by pushing forward $(2\pi)^{-2}\,\mathrm d\theta$ to $\Gamma_i$ by its collar parametrization.* *There is a scalar conductivity $\gamma_B$, bounded above and bounded away from zero, with the following property. For every $c=(c_0,c_1,c_2)\in\mathbb R^3$, the weak $\gamma_B$-harmonic function $U_c$ with boundary value $c_i$ on $\Gamma_i$ satisfies $$\label{bl:constant-flux} \int_B\gamma_B\nabla U_c\cdot\nabla\psi\,\,\mathrm dx =\sum_{i=0}^2 I_i(c)\int_{\Gamma_i}\psi\,\,\mathrm d\mu_i, \qquad \psi\in H^1(B),$$ where $\sum_i I_i(c)=0$. The linear map $c\mapsto I(c)$ has kernel $\mathbb R(1,1,1)$ and image $\{I\in\mathbb R^3:\sum_i I_i=0\}$.
  - Explanation: A three-boundary torus block carries a bounded positive scalar conductivity for which every separately constant voltage vector produces constant flux densities against prescribed probability measures. The current map has the common constants as kernel and the zero-sum plane as image.
- **claim-end-matching** (construction, [source](#stmt-bl-end-matching)): Fix one end after the preceding affine and angular normalizations, and write $A_0=\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(1,S)$ in its coordinates $(t,x,y)$. Let $u_{\rm in},v_{\rm in}$ be the two flat continuations in Equation <a href="#bl:flat-extension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="bl:flat-extension">[bl:flat-extension]</a>, with zero constant offsets and respective slopes $1,0$. Suppose first that $v_{\rm in}$ is nonzero, with leading term $b e^{-\lambda t}\cos(kx)$ as in Equation <a href="#bl:leading-mode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="bl:leading-mode">[bl:leading-mode]</a>; thus $\lambda^2=k^2S_{xx}$.* *For every sufficiently large $T$, there are smooth fields $u_T,v_T$ on $\mathbb T^2\times[T,T+4]$ and a smooth symmetric matrix $H_T$ compactly supported in the interior of this band such that $$\label{bl:end-matching-equations} \begin{gathered} \mathop{\mathrm{div}}\bigl((A_0+H_T)\nabla u_T\bigr)= \mathop{\mathrm{div}}\bigl((A_0+H_T)\nabla v_T\bigr)=0,\\ \left\lVert H_T\right\rVert_{L^\infty}\longrightarrow0\qquad(T\to\infty). \end{gathered}$$ Near the left edge the pair is exactly $(u_{\rm in},v_{\rm in})$; near the right edge it is exactly $(t,b e^{-\lambda t}\cos(kx))$. The fluxes in the positive $t$ direction there are respectively $(\partial_tu_{\rm in},\partial_tv_{\rm in})$ and $(1,-\lambda b e^{-\lambda t}\cos(kx))$. For large $T$, the corrected tensor $A_0+H_T$ is uniformly elliptic. The construction also has $\partial_tu_T>0$, and the two gradients are independent off a union of $2k$ smooth walls. If $v_{\rm in}$ is identically zero, one instead obtains $v_T=0$, right-hand field and flux pairs $(t,0)$ and $(1,0)$, and all the stated conclusions for $H_T,u_T$.
  - Explanation: On a finite band of a flat end, the asymptotic two-field pair is exactly matched to a single-mode target pair by a small compactly supported symmetric tensor correction, with ellipticity kept and independent gradients off finitely many walls.
- **claim-regular-correction** (theorem, [source](#stmt-bl-regular-correction)): Suppose $u,v$ are smooth with independent gradients on a connected open region. Let $r^1,r^2$ be smooth densities supported in a fixed compact subset of that region and satisfying $$\label{bl:three-moments} \int r^1=\int r^2= \int(vr^1-ur^2)=0.$$ Then there is a compactly supported smooth symmetric matrix density $H$ such that $$\label{bl:correction-equations} \mathop{\mathrm{div}}(H\nabla u)=r^1,\qquad \mathop{\mathrm{div}}(H\nabla v)=r^2.$$ For a fixed finite system of regular coordinate charts and supports, the construction has bounds in smooth norms. These bounds persist under sufficiently small smooth perturbations of the charts and fields.
  - Explanation: On a connected region with independent gradients, two smooth source densities satisfying three vanishing moment conditions are exactly produced by the divergences of a compactly supported symmetric matrix density applied to the two gradients, with stable bounds.
- **claim-block-geometry** (theorem, [source](#stmt-nu-geometry)): The three boundary components of $B$ are disjoint Lipschitz tori. They have compatible piecewise smooth bi-Lipschitz product collars, whose removal leaves a connected Lipschitz central region. Moreover, $\overline{D_-}$ and $\overline{D_+}$ are disjoint compact subsets of $D$.
  - Explanation: The nested prism geometry has three disjoint Lipschitz torus boundary components with compatible collars, a connected central region, and two child closures that are disjoint compact subsets of the parent.
- **claim-source-lemma** (theorem, [source](#stmt-nu-source-lemma)): There is a constant $K$, independent of $\nu$, such that for every $p=(p_0,p_-,p_+)$ with $p_0+p_-+p_+=0$, there exists $W_{\nu,p}\in H^1_0(\Omega)\cap L^\infty(\Omega)$, vanishing off $\overline{D_\nu}$ and constant on each child cell, with $$\label{nu:source-identity} \int_\Omega\gamma\nabla W_{\nu,p}\cdot\nabla\psi =p_0\int_{\Sigma_\nu}\operatorname{Tr}\psi\,\,\mathrm d\mu_\nu +p_-\int_{\Sigma_{\nu-}}\operatorname{Tr}\psi\,\,\mathrm d\mu_{\nu-} +p_+\int_{\Sigma_{\nu+}}\operatorname{Tr}\psi\,\,\mathrm d\mu_{\nu+}$$ for every $\psi\in H^1(\Omega)$. It obeys $$\label{nu:source-bounds} \left\lVert W_{\nu,p}\right\rVert_\infty\le K s^{-\left\lvert \nu\right\rvert}\left\lvert p\right\rvert, \qquad \int_\Omega\left\lvert \nabla W_{\nu,p}\right\rvert^2 \le K s^{-\left\lvert \nu\right\rvert}\left\lvert p\right\rvert^2.$$
  - Explanation: For every zero-sum charge triple and every cell of the nested family there is a bounded potential supported in that cell closure whose weak source is the prescribed combination of surface measures. Its size scales geometrically with the depth of the cell.
- **claim-equal-averages** (theorem, [source](#stmt-nu-equal-averages)): If $u\in H^1(\Omega)$ is weakly $\gamma$-harmonic, then $$m_\nu(u):=\int_{\Sigma_\nu}\operatorname{Tr}u\,\,\mathrm d\mu_\nu$$ has one common value $u_*$ for all finite words $\nu$. If $u$ is bounded, then $\left\lvert u_*\right\rvert\le\left\lVert u\right\rVert_\infty$.
  - Explanation: Every weakly harmonic function has the same trace average on all cell surfaces. For bounded functions this common value is bounded by the sup norm.
- **claim-potential-limit** (theorem, [source](#stmt-nu-potential)): The sequence $w^{(N)}$ converges in both $L^\infty(\Omega)$ and $H^1(\Omega)$ to a nonzero $w\in H^1_0(\Omega)\cap L^\infty(\Omega)$ that vanishes almost everywhere off $\overline D$.
  - Explanation: The partial sums of signed cell potentials converge uniformly and in the Sobolev norm to a nonzero bounded function in the zero-trace space vanishing off the closure of the parent prism.
- **claim-weighted-identity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-nu-weighted-identity)): Let $u$ be a bounded weakly $\gamma$-harmonic function in $\Omega$, and let $u_*$ be its common average from Lemma <a href="#nu:equal-averages" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nu:equal-averages">16</a>. Then, for every $\phi\in C_c^\infty(\Omega)$, $$\label{nu:weighted} \int_\Omega\gamma\nabla\rho\cdot \nabla\bigl((u-u_*)\phi\bigr)=0.$$
  - Explanation: The source of the limiting potential annihilates every product of a smooth compactly supported test function with the centered bounded harmonic function. This is the identity that allows the conductivity change to preserve boundary data.
- **claim-identical-dn** (theorem, [source](#stmt-nu-identical-dn)): The full weak Dirichlet-to-Neumann operators of $\gamma$ and $\widetilde\gamma$ on $\Omega$ are equal.
  - Explanation: The transformed conductivity yields the same full weak Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator as the original: for smooth boundary data the transformed solution is a harmonic extension with the same boundary pairing, and density extends equality to all traces.

        "anchor": "stmt-sc-local-join",
        "exact_quote": "Let $(A,E,F)$, with potentials $u_1,u_2$, be a subsolution. Let $y_0$ lie in one of its smooth patches from Definition <a href=\"#sc:subsolution\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"sc:subsolution\">5</a>, on which one fixed set of $m\\in\\{1,2\\}$ potentials has independent gradients. Suppose $A_0=A(y_0)$ is a coordinate join of two children $A^+,A^-$ in one orthonormal frame, with positive weights $\\theta_+,\\theta_-$ summing to one and with its joining path contained in $\\mathcal G$. Given open neighborhoods $\\mathcal O_+,\\mathcal O_-$ of the children in $\\mathrm{Sym}_3$ and $\\delta>0$, there is an open neighborhood $V$ of $y_0$, compactly contained in the patch, with the following property.*\n\n*For every open rectangular box $Q$ with $\\overline Q\\subset V$, one can choose a cutoff and periodic waveform, then construct subsolutions $(A_k',E_k',F_k')$, with potentials $u'_{1,k},u'_{2,k}$, for all sufficiently large integers $k$ such that $A_k'=A$ outside $Q$. The differences $u'_{j,k}-u_j$ and $F'_{j,k}-F_j$ vanish outside compact subsets of $Q$, and $$\\int_U(F'_{j,k}-F_j)\\cdot\\nabla\\psi\\,\\,\\mathrm dx=0\n \\qquad\\bigl(\\psi\\in H^1(U),\\ j=1,2\\bigr).$$ There are disjoint open subsets $Q_{k,+},Q_{k,-}\\subset Q$ such that $$A_k'\\in\\mathcal O_s\\ \\hbox{on }Q_{k,s}\\quad(s\\in\\{+,-\\}),\\qquad\n \\left\\lvert Q\\setminus(Q_{k,+}\\cup Q_{k,-})\\right\\rvert<\\delta\\left\\lvert Q\\right\\rvert.$$ On the same fixed box, with the cutoff and waveform fixed, $$(E_k',F_k')\\rightharpoonup(E,F)\n \\quad\\text{in }L^2(U)\\times L^2(U)\\quad\\text{as }k\\to\\infty.$$ The neighborhood and frequency threshold may depend on all preceding local data, including $\\mathcal O_+,\\mathcal O_-$ and $\\delta$; the threshold may also depend on the chosen box, cutoff, and waveform.",
        "source_id": "PM-365.3"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-sc-local-join",
          "exact_quote": "Let $(A,E,F)$, with potentials $u_1,u_2$, be a subsolution. Let $y_0$ lie in one of its smooth patches from Definition <a href=\"#sc:subsolution\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"sc:subsolution\">5</a>, on which one fixed set of $m\\in\\{1,2\\}$ potentials has independent gradients. Suppose $A_0=A(y_0)$ is a coordinate join of two children $A^+,A^-$ in one orthonormal frame, with positive weights $\\theta_+,\\theta_-$ summing to one and with its joining path contained in $\\mathcal G$. Given open neighborhoods $\\mathcal O_+,\\mathcal O_-$ of the children in $\\mathrm{Sym}_3$ and $\\delta>0$, there is an open neighborhood $V$ of $y_0$, compactly contained in the patch, with the following property.*\n\n*For every open rectangular box $Q$ with $\\overline Q\\subset V$, one can choose a cutoff and periodic waveform, then construct subsolutions $(A_k',E_k',F_k')$, with potentials $u'_{1,k},u'_{2,k}$, for all sufficiently large integers $k$ such that $A_k'=A$ outside $Q$. The differences $u'_{j,k}-u_j$ and $F'_{j,k}-F_j$ vanish outside compact subsets of $Q$, and $$\\int_U(F'_{j,k}-F_j)\\cdot\\nabla\\psi\\,\\,\\mathrm dx=0\n \\qquad\\bigl(\\psi\\in H^1(U),\\ j=1,2\\bigr).$$ There are disjoint open subsets $Q_{k,+},Q_{k,-}\\subset Q$ such that $$A_k'\\in\\mathcal O_s\\ \\hbox{on }Q_{k,s}\\quad(s\\in\\{+,-\\}),\\qquad\n \\left\\lvert Q\\setminus(Q_{k,+}\\cup Q_{k,-})\\right\\rvert<\\delta\\left\\lvert Q\\right\\rvert.$$ On the same fixed box, with the cutoff and waveform fixed, $$(E_k',F_k')\\rightharpoonup(E,F)\n \\quad\\text{in }L^2(U)\\times L^2(U)\\quad\\text{as }k\\to\\infty.$$ The neighborhood and frequency threshold may depend on all preceding local data, including $\\mathcal O_+,\\mathcal O_-$ and $\\delta$; the threshold may also depend on the chosen box, cutoff, and waveform.",
          "source_id": "PM-365.3"
        }

Suppose $A_0=A(y_0)$ is a coordinate join of two children $A^+,A^-$ in one orthonormal frame, with positive weights $\\theta_+,\\theta_-$ summing to one and with its joining path contained in $\\mathcal G$.         "anchor": "stmt-prop-branching-block",
        "exact_quote": "Let $B\\subset\\mathbb R^3$ be a bounded connected Lipschitz domain whose boundary has three components $\\Gamma_0,\\Gamma_1,\\Gamma_2$. Suppose these components have disjoint product collars parametrized by $\\mathbb T^2\\times[0,\\eta_i]$, where $\\mathbb T^2=(\\mathbb R/2\\pi\\mathbb Z)^2$. Assume the parametrizations are bi-Lipschitz, smooth with nonsingular derivative on open pieces covering up to a null set, and that removing sufficiently thin collars leaves a connected Lipschitz central region. Let $\\mu_i$ be the probability measure obtained by pushing forward $(2\\pi)^{-2}\\,\\mathrm d\\theta$ to $\\Gamma_i$ by its collar parametrization.*\n\n\n*There is a scalar conductivity $\\gamma_B$, bounded above and bounded away from zero, with the following property. For every $c=(c_0,c_1,c_2)\\in\\mathbb R^3$, the weak $\\gamma_B$-harmonic function $U_c$ with boundary value $c_i$ on $\\Gamma_i$ satisfies $$\\label{bl:constant-flux}\n \\int_B\\gamma_B\\nabla U_c\\cdot\\nabla\\psi\\,\\,\\mathrm dx\n   =\\sum_{i=0}^2 I_i(c)\\int_{\\Gamma_i}\\psi\\,\\,\\mathrm d\\mu_i,\n \\qquad \\psi\\in H^1(B),$$ where $\\sum_i I_i(c)=0$. The linear map $c\\mapsto I(c)$ has kernel $\\mathbb R(1,1,1)$ and image $\\{I\\in\\mathbb R^3:\\sum_i I_i=0\\}$.",
        "source_id": "PM-365.3"
      },
      "evidence": [

For every $c=(c_0,c_1,c_2)\\in\\mathbb R^3$, the weak $\\gamma_B$-harmonic function $U_c$ with boundary value $c_i$ on $\\Gamma_i$ satisfies $$\\label{bl:constant-flux}\n \\int_B\\gamma_B\\nabla U_c\\cdot\\nabla\\psi\\,\\,\\mathrm dx\n   =\\sum_{i=0}^2 I_i(c)\\int_{\\Gamma_i}\\psi\\,\\,\\mathrm d\\mu_i,\n \\qquad \\psi\\in H^1(B),$$ where $\\sum_i I_i(c)=0$. The linear map $c\\mapsto I(c)$ has kernel $\\mathbb R(1,1,1)$ and image $\\{I\\in\\mathbb R^3:\\sum_i I_i=0\\}$.",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-bl-end-matching",
        "exact_quote": "Fix one end after the preceding affine and angular normalizations, and write $A_0=\\mathop{\\mathrm{diag}}(1,S)$ in its coordinates $(t,x,y)$. Let $u_{\\rm in},v_{\\rm in}$ be the two flat continuations in Equation <a href=\"#bl:flat-extension\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"bl:flat-extension\">[bl:flat-extension]</a>, with zero constant offsets and respective slopes $1,0$. Suppose first that $v_{\\rm in}$ is nonzero, with leading term $b e^{-\\lambda t}\\cos(kx)$ as in Equation <a href=\"#bl:leading-mode\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"bl:leading-mode\">[bl:leading-mode]</a>; thus $\\lambda^2=k^2S_{xx}$.*\n\n*For every sufficiently large $T$, there are smooth fields $u_T,v_T$ on $\\mathbb T^2\\times[T,T+4]$ and a smooth symmetric matrix $H_T$ compactly supported in the interior of this band such that $$\\label{bl:end-matching-equations}\n \\begin{gathered}\n \\mathop{\\mathrm{div}}\\bigl((A_0+H_T)\\nabla u_T\\bigr)=\n \\mathop{\\mathrm{div}}\\bigl((A_0+H_T)\\nabla v_T\\bigr)=0,\\\\\n \\left\\lVert H_T\\right\\rVert_{L^\\infty}\\longrightarrow0\\qquad(T\\to\\infty).\n \\end{gathered}$$ Near the left edge the pair is exactly $(u_{\\rm in},v_{\\rm in})$; near the right edge it is exactly $(t,b e^{-\\lambda t}\\cos(kx))$. The fluxes in the positive $t$ direction there are respectively $(\\partial_tu_{\\rm in},\\partial_tv_{\\rm in})$ and $(1,-\\lambda b e^{-\\lambda t}\\cos(kx))$. For large $T$, the corrected tensor $A_0+H_T$ is uniformly elliptic. The construction also has $\\partial_tu_T>0$, and the two gradients are independent off a union of $2k$ smooth walls. If $v_{\\rm in}$ is identically zero, one instead obtains $v_T=0$, right-hand field and flux pairs $(t,0)$ and $(1,0)$, and all the stated conclusions for $H_T,u_T$.",
        "source_id": "PM-365.3"
      },
      "evidence": [

          "anchor": "stmt-bl-end-matching",

For large $T$, the corrected tensor $A_0+H_T$ is uniformly elliptic. The construction also has $\\partial_tu_T>0$, and the two gradients are independent off a union of $2k$ smooth walls.         "anchor": "stmt-bl-regular-correction",
        "exact_quote": "Suppose $u,v$ are smooth with independent gradients on a connected open region. Let $r^1,r^2$ be smooth densities supported in a fixed compact subset of that region and satisfying $$\\label{bl:three-moments}\n \\int r^1=\\int r^2=\n \\int(vr^1-ur^2)=0.$$ Then there is a compactly supported smooth symmetric matrix density $H$ such that $$\\label{bl:correction-equations}\n \\mathop{\\mathrm{div}}(H\\nabla u)=r^1,\\qquad \\mathop{\\mathrm{div}}(H\\nabla v)=r^2.$$ For a fixed finite system of regular coordinate charts and supports, the construction has bounds in smooth norms. These bounds persist under sufficiently small smooth perturbations of the charts and fields.",

      },

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Full weak Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator** ([source](#eq:weak-model)): Its full weak Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator is defined by
- **Tensor** ([source](#eq:energy-minimum)): A tensor is a measurable symmetric matrix $A$ with $aI\leq A\leq bI$ almost everywhere, where $0<a<b<\infty$.
- **Cauchy pair** ([source](#eq:normal-functional)): A Cauchy pair consists of $\operatorname{Tr}u$ and this functional for $F=A\nabla u$.
- **Diagonal laminate class** ([source](#pm-h-8)): Let $\mathcal G^{\mathrm{diag}}$ consist of the triples obtainable by a finite tree of these joins, starting from scalar triples $(s,s,s)$ with $a<s<b$.
- **Subsolution** ([source](#stmt-sc-subsolution)): A subsolution consists of a measurable symmetric matrix $A$, two potentials $u_1,u_2\in H^1(U)$, and the column matrices $$E=(\nabla u_1,\nabla u_2),\qquad F=AE,$$ such that $A\in\mathcal G$ almost everywhere and, for $j=1,2$, $$\begin{aligned} u_j-u_j^{\rm in}&\in H^1_0(U),& \mathop{\mathrm{div}}F_j&=0,\\ \int_U F_j\cdot\nabla\psi\,\,\mathrm dx &=\int_U F_j^{\rm in}\cdot\nabla\psi\,\,\mathrm dx &&\text{for every }\psi\in H^1(U). \end{aligned}$$ It must also have an open cover of a full-measure subset of $U$ on which $A,u_1,u_2$ are smooth and have the local rank property in Theorem <a href="#thm:scalarization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:scalarization">3</a>: on each member one fixed subset of the potentials has independent gradients, and every remaining potential is a constant affine combination of those potentials.
- **Angular decay rate** ([source](#bl:flat-extension)): Set $\lambda_h=(h^tSh)^{1/2}$.
- **Cell charges** ([source](#nu:charges)): Give the two first-level cells charges $q_-=1$ and $q_+=-1$, and recursively put $q_{\nu-}=q_{\nu+}=q_\nu/2$.

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-open-class** [claim](#stmt-sc-open-class) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sc-open-class): Explicit product leaves with arithmetic and harmonic means, inverse function theorem up a finite tree, continuity of ordered eigenvalues. -> openness and uniform coverage of the laminate class
- **step-polynomial-potential** [claim](#stmt-sc-polynomial-potential) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sc-polynomial-potential): Surjectivity of the symbol, adjugate formula for a polynomial potential, localization by a cutoff and oscillating primitive. -> exactly localized flux waves
- **step-symmetric-correction** [claim](#stmt-sc-symmetric-correction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sc-symmetric-correction): Direct algebraic verification with a pseudo-inverse. -> symmetric correction with controlled norm
- **step-local-join** [claim](#stmt-sc-local-join) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sc-local-join): Frozen-coefficient flux wave coupled to a normal diffeomorphism, symmetric correction of the constitutive defect, weak convergence by change of variables. -> localized subsolution near the children and weakly close
- **step-weak-approximation** [claim](#stmt-sc-weak-approximation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sc-weak-approximation): Finite-depth induction over laminate trees using grid boxes and the local join with a fixed volume tolerance. -> subsolution with nearly scalar matrix
- **step-baire** [claim](#stmt-sc-baire) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sc-baire): Baire category on closed sets defined by projection oscillation in a compact metric space. -> dense set of weak-to-strong continuity points
- **step-scalarization** [claim](#stmt-thm-scalarization) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sc-baire-2): Choose a continuity point, approximate by subsolutions with strong convergence, pass to a pointwise limit of the trace of the matrix. -> bounded measurable scalar conductivity with the same Cauchy pairs
- **step-regular-correction** [claim](#stmt-bl-regular-correction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-bl-regular-correction): Local potential coordinates, partition of unity, moment transfer along a spanning tree, compact divergence construction. -> compactly supported symmetric correction
- **step-end-matching** [claim](#stmt-bl-end-matching) / [proof](#proof-stmt-bl-regular-correction-2): Normalize and interpolate fields, remove sources near walls, transfer moments across walls, then solve in regular strips. -> exact matching band with small correction
- **step-branching-block** [claim](#stmt-prop-branching-block) / [proof](#pm-h-19): Attach flat ends, end matching, finite-distance termination of the mode, pushforward to collars, then scalarization of the two fields. -> scalar block with constant flux densities
- **step-geometry** [claim](#stmt-nu-geometry) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nu-geometry): Explicit coordinates of the boxes, explicit collars and a direct local graph check. -> Lipschitz geometry of the cell
- **step-source-lemma** [claim](#stmt-nu-source-lemma) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nu-source-lemma): Invert the flux map modulo constants on the block, extend by constants, scale by similarity. -> cell potentials with prescribed surface charges
- **step-equal-averages** [claim](#stmt-nu-equal-averages) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nu-equal-averages): Test the harmonic equation with the cell potentials and use orthogonality to the zero-sum plane. -> common average on all cell surfaces
- **step-potential-limit** [claim](#stmt-nu-potential) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nu-potential): Geometric level bounds, summability, and nontriviality by pairing with a separating test function. -> nonzero limiting potential
- **step-weighted-identity** [claim](#stmt-nu-weighted-identity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nu-weighted-identity): Pass to the limit in the finite source identity using a diameter estimate for the surface error. -> vanishing weighted source identity
- **step-identical-dn** [claim](#stmt-nu-identical-dn) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nu-identical-dn): Transform bounded harmonic extensions by the density rho, check the flux is divergence free, compare pairings with an outer-collar test, extend by density. -> equal full operators
- **step-main-theorem** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nu-identical-dn-2): Combine the geometry, the block, the nonzero potential and the identical operators. -> two distinct conductivities with one operator
- **step-localized-corollary** [claim](#stmt-cor-localized) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nu-identical-dn-3): Rescale the pair into separated balls and minimize energy over interface traces. -> many distinct conductivities with one operator

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-leaf-means**: For a target positive triple with a scale M above all its entries, each factor t_i taking its two values with equal weights has arithmetic mean one and harmonic mean alpha_i/M, which fixes the leaf scalars M t1 t2 t3.
- **inv-join-triple**: Joining the eight scalar leaves successively in directions one, two and three, with equal weights, returns exactly the prescribed diagonal triple.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-full-dn-from-scalarization**: Treat the scalar-leaf product tree as a way to replace an anisotropic matrix conductivity by a scalar one with the same full boundary map. -> The construction only reproduces the joined diagonal triple pointwise; it says nothing about the full Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator of a matrix conductivity.
- **anti-scale-not-above-target**: Run the leaf construction with a scale that is not larger than every entry of the target triple. -> The square root defining d_i is not a real number in (0,1), so the leaves and the equal-weight identity break down.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The product-leaf tree in the proof of the openness lemma is a finite explicit construction: eight scalar leaves and three rounds of equal-weight coordinate joins whose output can be computed in exact rational arithmetic and compared with the prescribed triple.

```python
# INPUTS: one line 'M d1 d2 d3' of exact rationals; d_i in (0,1), M > 0 (target alpha_i = M(1-d_i^2)).
# PRECONDITIONS: 0 < d_i < 1 and M > 0 so that every leaf is a positive scalar.
# OUTPUTS: one line: the three joined diagonal entries, then the smallest and largest leaf scalar, all exact fractions.
# TIME: constant (eight leaves, seven joins).
# SPACE: constant.
# ARITHMETIC: exact rational arithmetic via fractions.Fraction, no rounding.
# FAILURE: malformed input raises an exception (nonzero exit); no silent fallback.
import sys
from fractions import Fraction as F

def join(i, al, be):
    out = []
    for l in range(3):
        if l == i:
            out.append(1/(F(1,2)/al[l]+F(1,2)/be[l]))
        else:
            out.append(F(1,2)*al[l]+F(1,2)*be[l])
    return tuple(out)

def main():
    tok = sys.stdin.read().split()
    M = F(tok[0])
    d = [F(x) for x in tok[1:4]]
    ts = [(1-x,1+x) for x in d]
    leaves = {}
    for a in (0,1):
        for b in (0,1):
            for c in (0,1):
                s = M*ts[0][a]*ts[1][b]*ts[2][c]
                leaves[(a,b,c)] = (s,s,s)
    L1 = {}
    for b in (0,1):
        for c in (0,1):
            L1[(b,c)] = join(0, leaves[(0,b,c)], leaves[(1,b,c)])
    L2 = {}
    for c in (0,1):
        L2[c] = join(1, L1[(0,c)], L1[(1,c)])
    fin = join(2, L2[0], L2[1])
    lo = min(l[0] for l in leaves.values())
    hi = max(l[0] for l in leaves.values())
    print(" ".join(str(x) for x in fin), str(lo), str(hi))

main()
```

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper proves an existence result for bounded measurable conductivities. It gives no executable procedure, so no software component follows from it. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The zero-frequency electrostatic model has no dynamics or interactive content. The limiting construction is an infinite nested set, not something a simulation can realise. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the paper supplies a finite procedure that a tool could wrap. The proofs rely on Baire category and infinite limits. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The main results are existential. Scalarization uses a Baire continuity-point argument and the potential uses an infinite series, so no terminating algorithm with complexity is given. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No data structure is introduced. The nested cell indexing by finite words is a proof device for an infinite set. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper contains no computation to accelerate, only analytic constructions in function spaces. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No hardness assumption, protocol or primitive appears. Nonuniqueness of boundary data is a statement about an inverse problem, not about a cryptographic scheme. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper has no learning setting. It does show that boundary data alone cannot determine a bounded measurable conductivity, which limits any learned inverse without extra priors. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Energy minimisation appears only as a proof tool for weak solutions. No optimisation problem is posed or solved. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No distributed computation, consensus or communication appears. |
| other (inverse problems theory (negative uniqueness result)) | True | moderate | inspirational | inferred | The result is a counterexample showing that full-boundary data does not identify a bounded measurable scalar conductivity in three dimensions. It warns anyone relying on uniqueness at that regularity and is conceptual, not constructive. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-bounded-measurable-class** (hypothesis): The nonuniqueness is proved for scalar conductivities that are only bounded measurable and uniformly positive, with identical full weak Dirichlet-to-Neumann operators. It says nothing about conductivities with extra regularity, where uniqueness theorems are cited. Blocked use: ['Using the result to claim nonuniqueness for smooth or Lipschitz conductivities', 'Using the result to claim that every bounded measurable conductivity has a distinct partner with the same boundary operator']
- **limit-three-dimensions-ball** (edge_case): The construction is carried out on a ball in three dimensions, and the localized version in bounded connected Lipschitz domains of three-dimensional space. The text asserts no construction in other dimensions and in particular none in the plane. Blocked use: ['Applying the counterexample to planar problems', 'Extending the counterexample to higher dimensions without a new construction']
- **limit-finite-field-scalarization** (formal_scope): Scalarization preserves the Cauchy data of two designated solutions only, not the full operator of the matrix conductivity. A further argument is needed to reach every input. Blocked use: ['Replacing an anisotropic conductivity by a scalar one and expecting the same full boundary operator', 'Using the scalarization theorem as a general isotropization of matrix conductivities']
- **limit-scalarization-bounds** (approximation): The ellipticity bounds of the scalar conductivity are determined by the original bounds only through the class construction, and nothing is claimed about contrast or sharpness. Blocked use: ['Reading the bounds as optimal or as a prescribed contrast ratio']
- **limit-local-rank-cover** (hypothesis): Scalarization requires an open cover of a full-measure set on which the matrix and both potentials are smooth with the stated rank property. Fields without such a cover are outside the theorem. Blocked use: ['Applying the scalarization theorem to rough matrix fields without a smooth rank cover', 'Applying it to potentials whose gradients are dependent on a set of positive measure without an affine relation']
- **limit-baire-nonconstructive** (nonconstructive): The strong limit is selected through continuity points of a Baire argument. This gives existence of a dense set, not an explicit choice, so the scalar conductivity is not computed by any stated procedure. Blocked use: ['Presenting the scalarization step as an algorithm', 'Quoting convergence rates or complexity for the selected limit']
- **limit-local-join-threshold** (edge_case): In the local join step the frequency threshold depends on the chosen box, cutoff and waveform, so there is no uniform frequency valid for all boxes. Blocked use: ['Using one fixed oscillation frequency for all boxes of a grid', 'Claiming uniform-in-box constants for the perturbation']
- **limit-block-geometry-hypotheses** (hypothesis): The branching block requires three boundary tori with disjoint product collars, bi-Lipschitz parametrizations and a connected central region. Domains without this structure are not covered. Blocked use: ['Applying the block proposition to domains with different boundary topology', 'Applying it without verifying the collar and connectedness conditions']
- **limit-end-matching-large-T** (edge_case): End matching is asserted only for sufficiently large axial position, and its constants are for the fixed end. No estimate uniform in the leading frequency data is claimed. Blocked use: ['Using the matching band at small axial position', 'Transferring the smallness estimate to other ends with different leading modes']
- **limit-bounded-harmonic-only** (hypothesis): The weighted source identity is proved for bounded weakly harmonic functions only. The transformation of solutions is used for bounded solutions and extended to all data by density. Blocked use: ['Applying the transformation directly to unbounded harmonic functions', 'Dropping boundedness of the harmonic function in the identity']
- **limit-potential-support** (edge_case): The limiting potential is defined as a limit in uniform and Sobolev norms and vanishes outside the closure of the parent prism. No pointwise value on the nested limit set is used. Blocked use: ['Reading pointwise values of the potential on the nested null set', 'Treating the potential as given by a finite sum']
- **limit-fixed-numerical-parameters** (formal_scope): The numerical parameters of the cell geometry are one fixed choice that satisfies the two convergence inequalities. They are not shown to be unique or optimal. Blocked use: ['Treating the listed ratio as the only admissible one', 'Using the volume factor for other ratios without rechecking the inequalities']

### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Scope note of family 365 (`lean/docs/365.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [Nonuniqueness for Bounded Measurable Scalar Conductivities in Three Dimensions](../../preprints/Nonuniqueness-for-Bounded-Measurable-Scalar-Conductivities-in-Three-Dimensions-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The Calderón inverse problem asks whether boundary measurements determine an interior conductivity. The formalized result gives nonuniqueness for bounded measurable scalar conductivities on the ball $B(0,3)\subset\mathbb R^3$: two uniformly positive conductivities differ on a set of positive volume, equal $1$ near the boundary, and have the same full weak Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator. Weak solutions exist uniquely for every boundary trace.

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

noncomputable section
open MeasureTheory
open scoped ENNReal

namespace ScalarConductivity

abbrev R3 := EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin 3)
abbrev JetFiber := EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin 4)
def ball : Set R3 := Metric.ball 0 3
def ballMeasure : Measure R3 := volume.restrict ball
abbrev JetSpace := Lp JetFiber 2 ballMeasure

def smoothJet (f : R3 → ℝ) (x : R3) : JetFiber :=
  WithLp.toLp 2 (Fin.cases (f x) (fun i => gradient f x i))

def smoothJets : Set JetSpace :=
  { z | ∃ (f : R3 → ℝ) (_ : ContDiff ℝ (↑(⊤ : ℕ∞)) f)
      (hf : MemLp (smoothJet f) 2 ballMeasure), z = hf.toLp (smoothJet f) }

def compactSmoothJets : Set JetSpace :=
  { z | ∃ (f : R3 → ℝ) (_ : ContDiff ℝ (↑(⊤ : ℕ∞)) f)
      (_ : HasCompactSupport f) (_ : tsupport f ⊆ ball)
      (hf : MemLp (smoothJet f) 2 ballMeasure), z = hf.toLp (smoothJet f) }

def H1Space : Submodule ℝ JetSpace :=
  (Submodule.span ℝ smoothJets).topologicalClosure
abbrev H1 := H1Space

def zeroTraceAmbient : Submodule ℝ JetSpace :=
  (Submodule.span ℝ compactSmoothJets).topologicalClosure

def H10 : Submodule ℝ H1 := zeroTraceAmbient.comap H1Space.subtype

instance H10_isClosed : IsClosed (H10 : Set H1) := by
  exact (Submodule.isClosed_topologicalClosure
    (Submodule.span ℝ compactSmoothJets)).preimage continuous_subtype_val

abbrev TraceSpace := H1 ⧸ H10
abbrev TraceDual := TraceSpace →L[ℝ] ℝ
abbrev DNOperator := TraceSpace →L[ℝ] TraceDual

def trace (u : H1) : TraceSpace := H10.mkQ u

def weakGradient (u : H1) (x : R3) : R3 :=
  WithLp.toLp 2 (fun i => (u.val x) i.succ)

def energy (γ : R3 → ℝ) (u v : H1) : ℝ :=
  ∫ x, γ x * inner ℝ (weakGradient u x) (weakGradient v x) ∂ballMeasure

def Harmonic (γ : R3 → ℝ) (u : H1) : Prop :=
  ∀ h : H1, h ∈ H10 → energy γ u h = 0

def IsDirichletToNeumann (γ : R3 → ℝ) (Λ : DNOperator) : Prop :=
  ∀ f : TraceSpace, ∃ u : H1,
    trace u = f ∧ Harmonic γ u ∧
    (∀ u' : H1, trace u' = f → Harmonic γ u' → u' = u) ∧
    ∀ v : H1, Λ f (trace v) = energy γ u v

def EqualOneNearBoundary (γ : R3 → ℝ) : Prop :=
  ∃ r : ℝ, 0 < r ∧ r < 3 ∧ ∀ᵐ x ∂ballMeasure, r < ‖x‖ → γ x = 1

def MainStatement : Prop :=
  ∃ (γ₀ γ₁ : R3 → ℝ) (c C : ℝ) (Λ : DNOperator),
    0 < c ∧ c < C ∧
    Measurable γ₀ ∧ Measurable γ₁ ∧
    MemLp γ₀ ∞ ballMeasure ∧ MemLp γ₁ ∞ ballMeasure ∧
    (∀ᵐ x ∂ballMeasure, c ≤ γ₀ x ∧ γ₀ x ≤ C) ∧
    (∀ᵐ x ∂ballMeasure, c ≤ γ₁ x ∧ γ₁ x ≤ C) ∧
    EqualOneNearBoundary γ₀ ∧ EqualOneNearBoundary γ₁ ∧
    0 < ballMeasure {x | γ₀ x ≠ γ₁ x} ∧
    IsDirichletToNeumann γ₀ Λ ∧ IsDirichletToNeumann γ₁ Λ

theorem main_nonuniqueness : MainStatement := by
  sorry

end ScalarConductivity
end

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We construct two distinct uniformly positive bounded measurable scalar conductivities on a ball in ℝ<sup>3</sup> with the same full Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator. Both conductivities equal one near the boundary. This gives nonuniqueness in the scalar Calderón problem at bounded measurable regularity.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

<a id="eq:weak-model"></a>
Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a bounded connected Lipschitz domain and let $\gamma\in L^\infty(\Omega;\mathbb R)$ satisfy $0<c\leq\gamma\leq C<\infty$ almost everywhere. For $f\in H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)$, let $u_f\in H^1(\Omega)$ be the unique function of trace $f$ satisfying $$\label{eq:weak-model}
 \int_\Omega\gamma\nabla u_f\cdot\nabla\varphi\,\mathrm dx=0
 \qquad(\varphi\in H^1_0(\Omega)).$$ Its full weak Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator is defined by <a id="eq:dn-definition"></a>$$\label{eq:dn-definition}
 \left\langle \Lambda_\gamma f,g\right\rangle
 =\int_\Omega\gamma\nabla u_f\cdot\nabla v\,\mathrm dx,
 \qquad \operatorname{Tr}v=g.$$ Equation <a href="#eq:weak-model" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weak-model">[eq:weak-model]</a> makes this independent of the extension $v$. Our result concerns this entire operator on the usual trace space, at zero frequency. The inverse problem asks whether the boundary response to every applied voltage determines the conductivity inside the domain.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There exist real scalar functions $\gamma_0,\gamma_1\in
L^\infty(B(0,3);\mathbb R)$ and constants $0<c<C<\infty$ such that*

1.  *$c\leq\gamma_j\leq C$ almost everywhere, for $j=0,1$;*

2.  *both conductivities equal $1$ in a neighborhood of the boundary;*

3.  *$\left\lvert \{x:\gamma_0(x)\ne\gamma_1(x)\}\right\rvert>0$;*

4.  *$\Lambda_{\gamma_0}=\Lambda_{\gamma_1}$ as bounded operators from $H^{1/2}(\partial B(0,3))$ to $H^{-1/2}(\partial B(0,3))$.*

</div>

Thus the scalar Calderón uniqueness assertion with only bounded measurability and uniform positivity has a negative resolution, already in dimension three. In particular, the assertion quantified over every $n\geq3$ is false. The theorem imposes no derivative regularity and does not prescribe the contrast $C/c$ or assert a construction in every higher dimension.

<a id="stmt-cor-localized"></a>
<div id="cor:localized" class="corollary">

**Corollary 2** (Localized nonuniqueness). *Let $c,C$ be the constants in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. For every bounded connected Lipschitz domain $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^3$ and every finite family of pairwise disjoint balls $B_1,\ldots,B_m\Subset\Omega$, $m\geq1$, there are $2^m$ pairwise distinct real scalar conductivities $\gamma_\varepsilon\in L^\infty(\Omega)$, indexed by $\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}^m$, such that $$c\leq\gamma_\varepsilon\leq C\quad\text{almost everywhere},
 \qquad
 \gamma_\varepsilon=1\quad\text{on }
 \Omega\setminus\bigcup_{i=1}^m B_i,$$ and all their full weak Dirichlet-to-Neumann operators from $H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)$ to $H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega)$ agree. Distinct conductivities differ on a set of positive measure.*

</div>

The proof, given after the main construction, copies the pair into smaller separated balls and minimizes energy over their interface traces. It does not require the common response to be that of the constant conductivity one.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
###### Regularity and related constructions.

Calderón posed the determination problem for bounded measurable real scalar conductivities bounded away from zero, and proved injectivity of its linearization at constant conductivities <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Calderon](#ref-Calderon)]. For the nonlinear problem in dimensions at least three, Sylvester and Uhlmann established full-boundary uniqueness for smooth scalar conductivities <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[SylvesterUhlmann](#ref-SylvesterUhlmann)]. Later results reach lower regularity. Haberman proves uniqueness for uniformly elliptic $W^{1,n}$ conductivities in dimensions three and four <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Haberman](#ref-Haberman), Theorem 1.1]; Caro and Rogers prove it for positive Lipschitz conductivities in every dimension at least three <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[CaroRogers](#ref-CaroRogers), Theorem 1.1]. In the plane, Astala and Päivärinta prove uniqueness for bounded measurable uniformly positive scalar conductivities on bounded simply connected domains <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[AstalaPaivarinta](#ref-AstalaPaivarinta), Theorem 1]. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> concerns the three-dimensional measurable class, without the derivative hypotheses of the cited higher-dimensional results.

The companion scalar corollary proves uniqueness for strictly positive conductivities smooth up to the boundary of a bounded connected smooth domain in $\mathbb R^n$, $n\ge3$, with Dirichlet inputs supported in any nonempty relatively open boundary patch and conductivity flux observed on that same patch <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[OpenAISmoothPatch2026](#ref-OpenAISmoothPatch2026), Corollary 1.3].

For rough matrix-valued conductivities, nonunique continuation provides a route to inverse nonuniqueness. Daudé, Kamran, and Nicoleau <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[DaudeKamranNicoleau](#ref-DaudeKamranNicoleau), Theorem 1 and Proposition 1.2] combine Miller’s construction <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Miller](#ref-Miller)] with a conformal change to obtain anisotropic counterexamples with partial boundary data. Their full-boundary obstruction <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[DaudeKamranNicoleau](#ref-DaudeKamranNicoleau), Remark 1.3] also explains a difficulty for the present problem: a weakly harmonic factor with constant full boundary trace is itself constant by the energy identity. The factor used below has a nonzero interior source, subject to a more specific annihilation identity.

Scalar approximation of matrix conductivities raises a separate issue. Marino and Spagnolo <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[MarinoSpagnolo](#ref-MarinoSpagnolo)] establish isotropic density in the sense of $G$-convergence. Rondi, using an example communicated by Giovanni Alessandrini, makes the distinction between such approximation and exact boundary-map realization explicit <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[Rondi](#ref-Rondi), Proposition 2.2, Theorem 4.3, and Example 4.4]. On the unit disc, a constant matrix $\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(a,b)$ with $a,b>0$ and $a\ne b$ is an $H$-limit of scalar coefficients, with ellipticity bounds allowed to widen, but has no bounded uniformly positive scalar representative with the same full boundary map. The scalarization used here preserves exact responses to two prescribed inputs; a further argument is needed to reach every input.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
###### From a scalar block to the full operator.

The construction in Section <a href="#sec:nonuniqueness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:nonuniqueness">5</a> takes $\Omega=B(0,3)$ and nests scaled copies of a scalar block with one parent and two child boundary tori. Each torus carries a specified probability measure. The block can realize every current triple $p=(p_0,p_-,p_+)$ in the plane $p_0+p_-+p_+=0$, with flux equal to $p_i$ times the corresponding measure. After its parent voltage is normalized to zero, the potential extends by constants into the adjacent regions as an admissible compactly supported test for any global $\gamma$-harmonic function $u$. Reciprocity therefore gives $$p_0m_0(u)+p_-m_-(u)+p_+m_+(u)=0
 \qquad(p_0+p_-+p_+=0),$$ where $m_i(u)$ is the trace average on the indicated surface. The three averages are equal because their vector is orthogonal to the zero-sum plane. Iterating through the nested blocks gives one common average $u_*$ on every cell surface, for every global harmonic $u$.

Signed currents propagated through the cells produce a nonzero $w\in H^1_0(\Omega)\cap L^\infty(\Omega)$ supported away from the outer boundary. The common averages and shrinking cell diameters imply that its source satisfies $$\int_\Omega\gamma\nabla w\cdot
       \nabla\bigl((u-u_*)\phi\bigr)\,\mathrm dx=0
 \qquad\bigl(\phi\in C_c^\infty(\Omega)\bigr)$$ for every bounded weakly $\gamma$-harmonic $u$. The source is not zero; it vanishes on these particular products. This is the property used to change the conductivity.

Choose a sufficiently small nonzero $\delta$ so that $\rho=1+\delta w$ is positive, and set $$\widetilde\gamma=\rho^2\gamma,
 \qquad
 \widetilde u=u_*+\frac{u-u_*}{\rho}.$$ The corresponding flux is $$\widetilde\gamma\nabla\widetilde u
 =\rho\gamma\nabla u-(u-u_*)\gamma\nabla\rho.$$ The product identity, together with the original weak equation tested against $\rho\phi$, makes this flux divergence free. In the outer collar $\rho=1$, so the transformed solution and flux agree with the original ones. Smooth boundary voltages have bounded harmonic extensions, so their boundary responses agree; boundedness and density then give equality of the full operators on $H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)$. The nonzero potential makes the two positive conductivities distinct. Thus the source identity, applied to every bounded harmonic function, is the step from the block’s finite family of controlled inputs to the full inverse problem.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
###### Constructing the scalar block.

Section <a href="#sec:scalarization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:scalarization">3</a> proves an exact finite-field scalarization theorem. Under the stated local smoothness and rank conditions, it replaces a uniformly elliptic symmetric tensor by one bounded positive scalar coefficient while preserving the voltage trace and the entire normal-flux functional of each of two designated solutions. Here “finite-field” refers to the number of chosen inputs, not to a finite-dimensional observation of each response.

The product scalar leaves and successive coordinate joins have a classical antecedent in the isotropic approximation of Marino and Spagnolo <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[MarinoSpagnolo](#ref-MarinoSpagnolo), 3]. The localized flux waves use the polynomial potential method of Raiă ; the proof gives the explicit formula for a surjective symbol and the coupled construction required here. Together with a synchronized coordinate change and a small symmetric tensor correction, these waves produce successive perturbations satisfying the exact equations and preserving both boundary responses. A Baire continuity-point argument then selects a strong limit satisfying one scalar constitutive relation for both fields. This uses the method developed by Kirchheim <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Kirchheim](#ref-Kirchheim)] and adapted to elliptic equations by Astala, Faraco, and Székelyhidi <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[AstalaFaracoSzekelyhidi](#ref-AstalaFaracoSzekelyhidi), Lemmas 3.7–3.8]. These precedents supply methods; the simultaneous Cauchy-pair conclusion is proved in Section <a href="#sec:scalarization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:scalarization">3</a>.

Section <a href="#sec:block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:block">4</a> supplies the tensor and the two designated fields. It builds a block with one parent and two child boundary tori, and makes both fields affine in the normal coordinate at all three ends. The construction corrects small residuals across rank-one walls, then transfers a decaying mode through successively doubled angular frequencies until it vanishes at finite distance. The two resulting current vectors span the zero-sum plane. After scalarization, those two responses and the constant solution cover every separately constant voltage vector on the three boundary components. This is precisely the block property used to force the common surface averages above.

<a id="sec:prelim"></a>
### Weak equations and changes of variables

<a id="eq:energy-minimum"></a>
We use real functions throughout; complex trace spaces, if desired, follow by complex linear extension. Let $U\subset\mathbb R^3$ be bounded and Lipschitz. A tensor is a measurable symmetric matrix $A$ with $aI\leq A\leq bI$ almost everywhere, where $0<a<b<\infty$. The weak equation is $\mathop{\mathrm{div}}(A\nabla u)=0$, interpreted as in Equation <a href="#eq:weak-model" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weak-model">[eq:weak-model]</a>. The trace theorem, the zero-trace Poincaré inequality, and coercivity give existence and uniqueness for each trace. A weak solution minimizes its energy among functions of that trace: if $h\in H^1_0(U)$, then $$\label{eq:energy-minimum}
 \int_U A\nabla(u+h)\cdot\nabla(u+h)
 =\int_U A\nabla u\cdot\nabla u+
   \int_U A\nabla h\cdot\nabla h.$$ Testing positive and negative truncations gives the usual maximum bound when the trace is bounded. These facts require no derivative of $A$.

For a divergence-free $F\in L^2(U;\mathbb R^3)$, its normal flux is the continuous functional <a id="eq:normal-functional"></a>$$\label{eq:normal-functional}
 \left\langle F\cdot\nu,\operatorname{Tr}v\right\rangle
 :=\int_U F\cdot\nabla v\,\mathrm dx\qquad(v\in H^1(U)).$$ It is well defined because the integral vanishes for zero-trace functions. A Cauchy pair consists of $\operatorname{Tr}u$ and this functional for $F=A\nabla u$. This convention also fixes all signs when a function on a block is extended by constants across its boundary components.

We will use that matching traces glue $H^1$ functions across Lipschitz interfaces. Also $H^1\cap L^\infty$ is an algebra, with the ordinary weak product rule; Lipschitz compositions on the range of a function obey the weak chain rule. In particular truncation commutes with the trace, so the trace of a bounded $H^1$ function inherits its bounds. All interfaces used for these operations are finite unions of Lipschitz pieces; infinite constructions are passed to the limit in $H^1$ explicitly.

For later reference, let $x=X(y)$ be a bi-Lipschitz change of variables that is smooth with nonsingular derivative on open pieces covering almost every point. Write $J=DX$ and set, at $x=X(y)$, <a id="eq:pushforward"></a>$$\label{eq:pushforward}
 \widehat u(x)=u(y),\qquad
 \widehat A(x)=\frac{J A(y)J^t}{\left\lvert \det J\right\rvert},\qquad
 \widehat F(x)=\frac{J F(y)}{\left\lvert \det J\right\rvert}.$$ Then $\nabla\widehat u=J^{-t}\nabla u$, and change of variables gives <a id="eq:pushforward-test"></a>$$\label{eq:pushforward-test}
 \int_{X(U)}\widehat F\cdot\nabla\phi\,\mathrm dx
 =\int_U F\cdot\nabla(\phi\circ X)\,\mathrm dy.$$ Thus divergence-free fluxes remain divergence free, and the tensor rule preserves the weak energy pairing. A right-hand side transforms as a density: $\widehat r(X(y))=r(y)/\left\lvert \det J(y)\right\rvert$. Symmetry and uniform ellipticity persist, with bounds depending on the bi-Lipschitz constants. If $X$ is the identity outside an interior box, both Cauchy data are unchanged. We will apply the same rules to potential coordinates and to the piecewise smooth physical collars.

<a id="sec:scalarization"></a>
### Exact scalarization of two Cauchy pairs

This section replaces a matrix conductivity by a scalar one while preserving two specified Cauchy pairs exactly. This is a finite-field statement: it preserves the full flux response to each of two prescribed voltage inputs, not the matrix conductivity’s full Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator. Classical isotropic approximation in $G$-convergence provides useful historical context <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[MarinoSpagnolo](#ref-MarinoSpagnolo)], but no homogenization result is used below. The exact boundary data follow from localized perturbations and a strong limit selected by the Baire theorem. The continuity-point method follows the Baire-category framework for differential inclusions developed by Kirchheim <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Kirchheim](#ref-Kirchheim)] and used for elliptic equations by Astala, Faraco, and Székelyhidi <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[AstalaFaracoSzekelyhidi](#ref-AstalaFaracoSzekelyhidi), Lemmas 3.7–3.8]. We give the complete localized construction and limiting argument for the simultaneous Cauchy-pair constraints needed here.

<a id="stmt-thm-scalarization"></a>
<div id="thm:scalarization" class="theorem">

**Theorem 3** (Exact finite-field scalarization). *Let $U\subset\mathbb R^3$ be a bounded Lipschitz domain. Let $A$ be a measurable symmetric matrix field satisfying $$c_0I\leq A\leq C_0I\qquad\text{almost everywhere in }U,
 \qquad 0<c_0\leq C_0<\infty.$$ Suppose the real-valued potentials $u_1,u_2\in H^1(U)$ satisfy $\mathop{\mathrm{div}}(A\nabla u_j)=0$. Assume that there is an open cover of a full-measure subset of $U$ on whose members $A,u_1,u_2$ are smooth and have the following local rank property. On each member, a fixed subset of the potentials has everywhere independent gradients, and every remaining potential is a constant affine combination of that subset. The subset may have zero, one, or two elements; a zero-element subset means that both potentials are constant there.*

<a id="sc:conclusion-equations"></a>
<a id="sc:conclusion-flux"></a>
*There exist constants $0<a<b<\infty$, a measurable scalar $a\leq\gamma\leq b$, and $v_1,v_2\in H^1(U)$ such that $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathop{\mathrm{div}}(\gamma\nabla v_j)&=0,
 &v_j-u_j&\in H^1_0(U), \label{sc:conclusion-equations}\\
 \int_U\gamma\nabla v_j\cdot\nabla\psi\,\,\mathrm dx
 &=\int_U A\nabla u_j\cdot\nabla\psi\,\,\mathrm dx
 &&\text{for every }\psi\in H^1(U). \label{sc:conclusion-flux}
\end{aligned}$$ The bounds $a,b$ may be chosen using only $c_0,C_0$.*

</div>

We use the Euclidean norm for vectors and the Frobenius norm for matrices. Write $\mathrm{Sym}_3$ for the vector space of real symmetric $3\times3$ matrices, with this norm. Gradient and flux matrices have their fields as columns. In particular, $E^tF$ is the matrix of their pointwise pairings. The proof will not require a positive lower bound on the independent gradients throughout $U$: such bounds are used only on individual compactly contained coordinate boxes.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### An open class of finite laminates

For positive triples $\alpha,\beta\in(0,\infty)^3$, a coordinate direction $i$, and $0\leq\theta\leq1$, define their coordinate join $J_i(\theta;\alpha,\beta)$ by <a id="sc:join"></a>$$\label{sc:join}
 [J_i]_i=\left(\frac{\theta}{\alpha_i}
                  +\frac{1-\theta}{\beta_i}\right)^{-1},
 \qquad
 [J_i]_\ell=\theta\alpha_\ell+(1-\theta)\beta_\ell
 \quad(\ell\ne i).$$ Fix $0<a<b$. Let $\mathcal G^{\mathrm{diag}}$ consist of the triples obtainable by a finite tree of these joins, starting from scalar triples $(s,s,s)$ with $a<s<b$. A tree is evaluated in one fixed coordinate frame. Let $\mathcal G$ be its spectral extension: $$\mathcal G=
 \{Q\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(\alpha)Q^t:
       Q\text{ orthogonal},\ \alpha\in\mathcal G^{\mathrm{diag}}\}.$$ In particular, every matrix in $\mathcal G$ lies strictly between $aI$ and $bI$.

<a id="stmt-sc-open-class"></a>
<div id="sc:open-class" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Openness and coverage). *The set $\mathcal G^{\mathrm{diag}}$ is open in $\mathbb R^3$, and $\mathcal G$ is open in $\mathrm{Sym}_3$. Every joining path in a representing tree lies in $\mathcal G$. Given $c_0,C_0$, the numbers $a,b$ can be chosen so that every symmetric matrix with spectrum in $[c_0,C_0]$ belongs to $\mathcal G$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sc-open-class"><summary>Proof of stmt-sc-open-class</summary>

<a id="sc:product-leaves"></a>
*Proof.* The product construction below is related to the diagonal approximation in Marino and Spagnolo <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[MarinoSpagnolo](#ref-MarinoSpagnolo), Section 3]; we use its elementary coordinate-join arithmetic directly. First we show that each scalar triple strictly between the endpoints is interior. Given a nearby positive triple $(\alpha_1,\alpha_2,
\alpha_3)$, choose $M>\max_i\alpha_i$ and set $$d_i=\sqrt{1-\alpha_i/M}.$$ Let $t_i$ independently take the two values $1-d_i,1+d_i$ with equal weights. Its arithmetic mean is one and its harmonic mean is $1-d_i^2=\alpha_i/M$. Start with the eight scalar leaves $$\label{sc:product-leaves}
 M t_1t_2t_3I.$$ Joining the $t_1$ pairs in direction 1 gives the diagonal triple $$(\alpha_1t_2t_3,Mt_2t_3,Mt_2t_3).$$ Joining the $t_2$ pairs in direction 2 gives $(\alpha_1t_3,\alpha_2t_3,Mt_3)$, and the direction-3 joins give $(\alpha_1,\alpha_2,\alpha_3)$. If the target tends to $(s,s,s)$, choose $M$ tending to $s$ from above. Every leaf then tends to $s$, so all leaves lie in $(a,b)$ for sufficiently close targets.

Interior propagates through any nontrivial join. With the other child and a weight $0<\theta<1$ fixed, the derivative of the join in its first child is diagonal, with entries $\theta$ in the tangential positions and $\theta[J_i]_i^2/\alpha_i^2>0$ in the normal position. It is invertible. The inverse function theorem, applied successively up a finite tree, proves openness in diagonal variables. Zero-weight joins can be removed. The diagonal class is permutation invariant; continuity of ordered eigenvalues now proves that its spectral extension is open, including at repeated eigenvalues. Varying the weight of an existing join simply supplies another finite tree with the same children, so its entire path, including its endpoints, stays in $\mathcal G$.

For uniform coverage, take $M=2C_0$ in Equation <a href="#sc:product-leaves" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:product-leaves">[sc:product-leaves]</a>. For targets in $[c_0,C_0]^3$, all factors lie between $$\ell=1-\sqrt{1-c_0/(2C_0)}>0
 \quad\text{and}\quad 2.$$ All leaves therefore lie in $[M\ell^3,8M]$. For example, $a=M\ell^3/2$ and $b=16M$ put them strictly inside the leaf interval, uniformly for the entire required spectral range. ◻

</details>

Fix these $a,b$ for the rest of the proof. The distance of a joining path from $\mathrm{Sym}_3\setminus\mathcal G$ can depend on the path, but every fixed path is compact in the open set $\mathcal G$ and hence has positive such distance. All corrections below are symmetric and will respect that distance. Thus every corrected matrix remains in the same $\mathcal G$, and the global bounds $a,b$ never deteriorate.

We now fix the equations and boundary data that every intermediate field must preserve. Write $A^{\rm in},u_1^{\rm in},u_2^{\rm in}$ for the data of Theorem <a href="#thm:scalarization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:scalarization">3</a>, and put $F_j^{\rm in}=A^{\rm in}\nabla u_j^{\rm in}$.

<a id="stmt-sc-subsolution"></a>
<div id="sc:subsolution" class="definition">

**Definition 5** (Subsolution). A subsolution consists of a measurable symmetric matrix $A$, two potentials $u_1,u_2\in H^1(U)$, and the column matrices $$E=(\nabla u_1,\nabla u_2),\qquad F=AE,$$ such that $A\in\mathcal G$ almost everywhere and, for $j=1,2$, $$\begin{aligned}
 u_j-u_j^{\rm in}&\in H^1_0(U),& \mathop{\mathrm{div}}F_j&=0,\\
 \int_U F_j\cdot\nabla\psi\,\,\mathrm dx
 &=\int_U F_j^{\rm in}\cdot\nabla\psi\,\,\mathrm dx
 &&\text{for every }\psi\in H^1(U).
\end{aligned}$$ It must also have an open cover of a full-measure subset of $U$ on which $A,u_1,u_2$ are smooth and have the local rank property in Theorem <a href="#thm:scalarization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:scalarization">3</a>: on each member one fixed subset of the potentials has independent gradients, and every remaining potential is a constant affine combination of those potentials.

</div>

The original fields are a subsolution by Lemma <a href="#sc:open-class" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:open-class">4</a>. The construction will change the matrix and interior fields while keeping the equations and the two entire boundary responses in this definition.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### A split in preliminary coordinates

<a id="sc:preliminary-states"></a>
We first compute the states to be realized by a local perturbation. A divergence-free flux wave with the potentials held fixed can mix the tangential entries of a coordinate join. The normal entry requires its harmonic mean instead. We obtain both at once by coupling the flux wave to a normal change of variables; the following states describe that coupling before localization. Freeze a parent matrix $A_0$ and its two diagonal children $A^+$, $A^-$ in a common orthonormal frame. Let the layering direction be the unit vector $d$, and let the physical fractions be $\theta_+,\theta_->0$, with sum one. A trivial join needs no perturbation. Subscripts $d$ and $T$ denote the normal scalar entry and tangential diagonal block. Set $$\label{sc:preliminary-states}
 q_s=\frac{A_d^s}{(A_0)_d},\qquad
 B_d^s=(A_0)_d,\qquad B_T^s=q_s A_T^s,
 \qquad \eta_s=\frac{\theta_s}{q_s},
 \quad s\in\{+,-\}.$$ The normal harmonic-mean formula and the tangential arithmetic-mean formula give <a id="sc:means"></a>$$\label{sc:means}
 \eta_++\eta_-=1,\qquad
 \eta_+q_++\eta_-q_-=1,\qquad
 \eta_+B^++\eta_-B^-=A_0.$$ The fractions $\eta_s$, not $\theta_s$, are used before the coordinate change.

<a id="sc:synchronized-segment"></a>
Put $\Delta B=B^+-B^-$ and $\Delta q=q_+-q_-$. A scalar parameter $z\in[-\eta_+,\eta_-]$ describes the segment $$\label{sc:synchronized-segment}
 B(z)=A_0+z\Delta B,\qquad q(z)=1+z\Delta q.$$ Its endpoints are the two states in Equation <a href="#sc:preliminary-states" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:preliminary-states">[sc:preliminary-states]</a>; its barycenter is $z=0$. The normal stretch $L(z)=I+(q(z)-1)d\otimes d$ pushes $B(z)$ forward to <a id="sc:predicted-matrix"></a>$$\label{sc:predicted-matrix}
 C(z)=\frac{L(z)B(z)L(z)^t}{\det L(z)}
      =\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}\bigl(q(z)(A_0)_d,\ B_T(z)/q(z)\bigr)$$ in the chosen frame. This entire path is the original coordinate joining path, with changed weights. Indeed if $B=\sum_s\tau_s B^s$ and $q=\sum_s\tau_s q_s$ for $\tau_++\tau_-=1$, the physical weights are $w_s=\tau_s q_s/q$. Their normal harmonic mean is $$\left(\sum_s\frac{w_s}{A_d^s}\right)^{-1}
 =q(A_0)_d,$$ and their tangential arithmetic mean is $B_T/q$. In particular, smoothing and cutting off the scalar parameter while keeping it in its segment will not leave the admissible path.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Exact localized flux waves

Let $m\in\{1,2\}$ potentials have independent gradients on a smooth patch. Temporarily discard the affinely dependent columns. For a symmetric matrix to map their gradient matrix $E$ to a flux matrix $F$, the pairing $E^tF$ must be symmetric. The flux wave must preserve this condition as well as column divergence. After shrinking the patch, choose smooth coordinates $$z=\Phi(y),\qquad z_1=u_1(y),\ldots,z_m=u_m(y),$$ where the independent potentials have been relabeled if needed. If $J=D\Phi$, a flux column becomes the divergence density $J F/\left\lvert \det J\right\rvert$. In these coordinates, symmetry of $E^tF$ is exactly symmetry of the leading $m\times m$ block of this transformed flux matrix. Thus the two constraints to preserve are constant coefficient: column divergences vanish and that block is symmetric.

Let $V_m$ be the vector space of $3\times m$ matrices with symmetric leading block, equipped with the Frobenius inner product. Define $$D(\xi):V_m\longrightarrow\mathbb R^m,\qquad D(\xi)G=\xi^tG.$$ We identify the row vector on the right with a vector in $\mathbb R^m$.

The following is the surjective-symbol case of the polynomial potential construction of Raiă . We give the explicit formula used for these coupled constraints.

<a id="stmt-sc-polynomial-potential"></a>
<div id="sc:polynomial-potential" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6** (A polynomial potential). *For every $\xi\ne0$, $D(\xi)$ is onto. There is a homogeneous polynomial map $P(\xi):V_m\to V_m$ of degree $2m$ such that $$D(\xi)P(\xi)=0,\qquad
 \operatorname{im}P(\xi)=\ker D(\xi)\quad(\xi\ne0).$$ Consequently every mean-zero smooth periodic plane wave with polarization in $\ker D(\xi)$ admits a compactly supported localization satisfying both constraints exactly, with arbitrarily small absolute uniform norm of the difference from its cut-off leading wave.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sc-polynomial-potential"><summary>Proof of stmt-sc-polynomial-potential</summary>

*Proof.* For $m=1$, surjectivity is ordinary nonzero contraction. For $m=2$ write $$G=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\b&c\\d&e\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 D(\xi)G=(\xi_1a+\xi_2b+\xi_3d,
           \xi_1b+\xi_2c+\xi_3e).$$ If $\lambda\in\mathbb R^2$ annihilates its range, then $$\xi_1\lambda_1=\xi_2\lambda_2
 =\xi_1\lambda_2+\xi_2\lambda_1
 =\xi_3\lambda_1=\xi_3\lambda_2=0.$$ When $\xi_3\ne0$ the assertion follows immediately; otherwise either $\xi_1$ or $\xi_2$ is nonzero and the first three equalities give the same conclusion. Thus $D(\xi)D(\xi)^t$ is positive definite at every nonzero $\xi$, where the transpose uses the specified inner products.

Set $Q(\xi)=D(\xi)D(\xi)^t$ and define <a id="sc:polynomial-symbol"></a>$$\label{sc:polynomial-symbol}
 P(\xi)=\det Q(\xi)I
       -D(\xi)^t\operatorname{adj}(Q(\xi))D(\xi).$$ The identity $Q\operatorname{adj}Q=(\det Q)I$ gives $DP=0$ as a polynomial identity. On $\ker D(\xi)$, $P(\xi)$ is multiplication by the nonzero scalar $\det Q(\xi)$, proving the image assertion. Both terms have degree $2m$.

<a id="sc:localized-potential"></a>
For completeness, let $R\in\ker D(\xi)$ and choose $R_0\in V_m$ with $P(\xi)R_0=R$. Let $h$ be a smooth one-periodic function of mean zero and let $H$ be a one-periodic $2m$-fold primitive, so $H^{(2m)}=h$. Such primitives are obtained by successive integration and subtraction of the mean. Given a smooth compactly supported cutoff $\chi$, apply the constant coefficient differential operator $P(\partial)$ to $$\label{sc:localized-potential}
 k^{-2m}\chi(z)H(k\xi\cdot z)R_0.$$ Its output lies in $V_m$ and has zero column divergence exactly, by the polynomial identity. Terms with every derivative on $H$ give $\chi(z)h(k\xi\cdot z)R$. Each remaining term has at least one derivative on $\chi$ and is $O(k^{-1})$ uniformly for fixed $\chi,h$. This proves the localization claim. ◻

</details>

The flux wave will satisfy the differential constraints exactly but will have a small constitutive error. The following algebraic fact removes that error without losing symmetry.

<a id="stmt-sc-symmetric-correction"></a>
<div id="sc:symmetric-correction" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (Symmetric algebraic correction). *Let $E$ have independent columns and let $R$ satisfy that $E^tR$ is symmetric. With $E^\dagger=(E^tE)^{-1}E^t$, the matrix <a id="sc:correction-formula"></a>$$\label{sc:correction-formula}
 H=R E^\dagger+(E^\dagger)^tR^t
       -(E^\dagger)^t(E^tR)E^\dagger$$ is symmetric and satisfies $HE=R$. Its norm is bounded by a locally uniform constant times $\left\lVert R\right\rVert$ when $E$ ranges over a bounded set with its smallest singular value bounded below.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sc-symmetric-correction"><summary>Proof of stmt-sc-symmetric-correction</summary>

*Proof.* Symmetry is immediate. Multiply by $E$, use $E^\dagger E=I$, and cancel the last two terms using $R^tE=E^tR$. The norm bound follows from the same formula. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Realizing one coordinate join

We can now state the local operation used to exhaust a finite laminate tree. Its two conclusions serve different purposes: proximity to the children lowers the remaining tree depth, while weak proximity lets us make that change inside any prescribed weak neighborhood.

<a id="stmt-sc-local-join"></a>
<div id="sc:local-join" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8** (Local realization of a coordinate join). *Let $(A,E,F)$, with potentials $u_1,u_2$, be a subsolution. Let $y_0$ lie in one of its smooth patches from Definition <a href="#sc:subsolution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:subsolution">5</a>, on which one fixed set of $m\in\{1,2\}$ potentials has independent gradients. Suppose $A_0=A(y_0)$ is a coordinate join of two children $A^+,A^-$ in one orthonormal frame, with positive weights $\theta_+,\theta_-$ summing to one and with its joining path contained in $\mathcal G$. Given open neighborhoods $\mathcal O_+,\mathcal O_-$ of the children in $\mathrm{Sym}_3$ and $\delta>0$, there is an open neighborhood $V$ of $y_0$, compactly contained in the patch, with the following property.*

*For every open rectangular box $Q$ with $\overline Q\subset V$, one can choose a cutoff and periodic waveform, then construct subsolutions $(A_k',E_k',F_k')$, with potentials $u'_{1,k},u'_{2,k}$, for all sufficiently large integers $k$ such that $A_k'=A$ outside $Q$. The differences $u'_{j,k}-u_j$ and $F'_{j,k}-F_j$ vanish outside compact subsets of $Q$, and $$\int_U(F'_{j,k}-F_j)\cdot\nabla\psi\,\,\mathrm dx=0
 \qquad\bigl(\psi\in H^1(U),\ j=1,2\bigr).$$ There are disjoint open subsets $Q_{k,+},Q_{k,-}\subset Q$ such that $$A_k'\in\mathcal O_s\ \hbox{on }Q_{k,s}\quad(s\in\{+,-\}),\qquad
 \left\lvert Q\setminus(Q_{k,+}\cup Q_{k,-})\right\rvert<\delta\left\lvert Q\right\rvert.$$ On the same fixed box, with the cutoff and waveform fixed, $$(E_k',F_k')\rightharpoonup(E,F)
